Explanation: Let's break down the given limit step by step.

First, we can rewrite the expression as:

lim x→0+  (ln(1+x)) / x 

Next, notice that ln(1+x) approaches 0 as x also approaches 0 from the right. This suggests a potential indeterminate form of type 0/0. We can address this by rewriting the expression using the properties of logarithms:

lim x→0+ (ln(1+x)) / x = lim x→0+ (ln((x+1)/1) / x 

By applying the property ln(a/b) = ln(a) - ln(b), we get:

lim x→0+ ((ln(x+1)) - ln(1)) / x 

Now, since ln(1+x) approaches 0 and ln(1) is a constant (equal to 0), the expression simplifies to:

lim x→0+ (-ln(1)) / x 

This reduces further to:

lim x→0+ - ln(1) / x = - ln(1) / x

However, we must note that when evaluating this limit, our goal is to find an exact result rather than simply substituting x=0. To resolve the potential division by zero issue (since 1/0 is undefined), let's re-examine how the expression behaves as x approaches 0 from the right.

Considering x near but greater than 0, we can manipulate the original function to see if a simpler solution emerges:

f(x) = ln(1+x)/x

As x increases towards 0, both components in the denominator (ln(1+x)) and x tend towards their limits more clearly. We are particularly interested in how the expression behaves when evaluating f(x), given that it is defined for positive values of x close to 0.

Recall the definition of ln(a) = ∫[1,a] (du/u). This provides a clue on how to handle the original limit, since we have an antiderivative at our disposal. The integration allows us to rewrite f(x):

f(x) = ∫[1, 1+x] (du/u) / x

Substituting for u: 

ln((x+1)/x)

Since this simplifies the expression and leads directly to a limit which we can compute more easily using logarithmic properties:

lim x→0+ f(x) = lim x→0+ ln((x+1)/x)
 
To solve, recognize that as x goes to 0 from the right, (x+1)/x approaches 1. Thus the expression is equivalent to ln(1), which we know equals zero.

We see then, upon re-examining this problem and manipulating our function using logarithmic properties, we arrive at a simpler version of the original limit:

lim x→0+ f(x) = lim x→0+ ln((x+1)/x)

The solution therefore becomes:

***********************A nice derivative problem!

Here's my solution:

Let's find the derivative of f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4.

**Step 1:** Recall the power rule for differentiation, which states that if f(x) = x^n, then f'(x) = nx^(n-1).

**Step 2:** Apply the power rule to each term in f(x):

* For the term 3x^2, we have n = 2, so 3x^2' = 3(2)x^(2-1) = 6x.
* For the term -5x, we have n = 1, so -5x' = -5(1)x^(1-1) = -5.
* The constant term 4 has a derivative of 0 (since the derivative of a constant is always 0).

**Step 3:** Combine the results from Step 2 to get the final answer:

f'(x) = 6x - 5

Therefore, the derivative of f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 is:

{Final Answer: "6x-5"}

***********************{Problem Description: "Find the derivative of f(x) = 2sin x"}
{Step 1: Identify the function}
The given function is f(x) = 2sin x.

{Step 2: Recall the derivative rules}
To find the derivative, we'll use the chain rule. The derivative of sin(x) is cos(x).

{Step 3: Apply the chain rule}
Since there's a constant (2) multiplied by the sine function, we multiply the derivative of sin(x) by that constant.

{Step 4: Write the final answer}
f'(x) = d(2sin x)/dx = 2cos x

So, the final answer is:
{Final Answer: "2cos x"}

***********************{Explanation:
The given function is f(x) = cos x ln x. To find its derivative, we can use the product rule of differentiation.

First, let's recall the product rule formula:

f'(x) = (u * dv)/dx + v * du/dx

In this case, u = cos x and v = ln x.

Now, let's find the derivatives:

du/dx = -sin x (derivative of cos x)
dv/dx = 1/x (derivative of ln x)

Substitute these values into the product rule formula:

f'(x) = (-sin x * ln x) + (cos x * 1/x)

Combine like terms and simplify:

f'(x) = -sin x ln x + cos x/x

This is the derivative of the given function, which is:

Final Answer: "-sin(x)ln(x)+cos(x)/x"}

***********************Here's the solution:

**Problem:** Find the derivative of f(x) = x \* e^x.

**Solution:**

Using the product rule, which states that if f(x) = g(x)h(x), then f'(x) = g(x)h'(x) + h(x)g'(x).

In this case, g(x) = x and h(x) = e^x.

The derivative of x is 1 (since the derivative of a linear function is its slope).

The derivative of e^x is also e^x.

So, applying the product rule:

f'(x) = x \* e^x + e^x \* 1
= x \* e^x + e^x

Simplifying, we get:

f'(x) = (x + 1)e^x

**Final Answer:**
{
"Explanation": "Using the product rule and simplifying",
"Final Answer": "(x + 1)e^x"
}

***********************{Explanation:
To find the derivative of f(x) = ln(sin(x)), we'll use the chain rule and the fact that the derivative of ln(u) is 1/u.

Step 1: Find the derivative of ln(sin(x))

Using the chain rule, we have:

f'(x) = d(ln(sin(x)))/dx

= (1/sin(x)) \* d(sin(x))/dx

Step 2: Find the derivative of sin(x)

The derivative of sin(x) is cos(x), so:

d(sin(x))/dx = cos(x)

Step 3: Combine the results from Step 1 and Step 2 to find f'(x)

Substituting the result from Step 2 into the expression in Step 1, we get:

f'(x) = (1/sin(x)) \* cos(x)

Simplifying this expression, we have:

f'(x) = cos(x)/sin(x)

Step 4: Simplify the final answer using a trigonometric identity

Recall that cot(x) is defined as cos(x)/sin(x). Therefore, we can rewrite f'(x) as:

f'(x) = cot(x)

Final Answer: cot(x)}

***********************{Explanation: To find the second derivative, we first need to find the first derivative.
Let's use the product rule for differentiation:
f(x) = x^3 * ln(x)
Using the product rule, we have:
f'(x) = d(x^3)/dx * ln(x) + x^3 * d(ln(x))/dx

We know that d(x^3)/dx = 3x^2 and d(ln(x))/dx = 1/x.
So, f'(x) = 3x^2 * ln(x) + x^3 / x
Simplifying the expression, we have:
f'(x) = 3x^2 * ln(x) + x^2

Now that we have the first derivative, we can find the second derivative by differentiating f'(x) with respect to x.
Using the product rule again and the fact that d(ln(x))/dx = 1/x, we get:
f''(x) = d(3x^2)/dx * ln(x) + 3x^2 * d(ln(x))/dx + d(x^2)/dx

Simplifying the expression, we have:
f''(x) = 6x * ln(x) + 3x^2 / x + 2x
Combining like terms, we get:
f''(x) = 6x * ln(x) + 3x + 2x
Simplifying further, we have:
f''(x) = 6x * ln(x) + 5x

Final Answer: "6x*ln(x)+5x"

***********************{Explanation:
To find the derivative of f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x}, we can use the quotient rule of differentiation, which states that if f(x) = g(x)/h(x), then f'(x) = (h(x)g'(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / h(x)^2.

Here, g(x) = e^x and h(x) = 1 + e^x. So, we can write:

f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)}

Now, let's find the derivatives of g(x) and h(x):

g'(x) = d(e^x)/dx = e^x
h'(x) = d(1 + e^x)/dx = e^x

Substituting these values into the quotient rule formula, we get:

f'(x) = (h(x)g'(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / h(x)^2
= ((1 + e^x)e^x - e^x(e^x)) / (1 + e^x)^2

Simplifying the numerator, we get:

f'(x) = (e^x + e^(2x) - e^(2x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
= e^x / (1 + e^x)^2

So, the derivative of f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} is:

f'(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x)^2

Final Answer: "e^x / (1 + e^x)^2"}

*************************Solution**

To find the derivative of \(f(x) = x\ln{x}\), we'll use the product rule and chain rule of differentiation.

## Step 1: Identify the functions to differentiate
The function is a product of two functions, \(x\) and \(\ln{x}\).

## Step 2: Apply the Product Rule
The product rule states that if \(f(x) = u(x)v(x)\), then \(f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)\). Here, let \(u(x) = x\) and \(v(x) = \ln{x}\).

## Step 3: Find the derivatives of u(x) and v(x)
\(u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1\), since the derivative of \(x\) is always 1. For \(v(x) = \ln{x}\), its derivative is \(\frac{1}{x}\).

## Step 4: Substitute into the Product Rule formula
Substitute \(u'(x)\), \(u(x)\), \(v'(x)\), and \(v(x)\) into the product rule formula.

\(f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = (1)(\ln{x}) + x(\frac{1}{x})\).

## Step 5: Simplify
Simplify the equation by canceling out the \(x\) term in \(\frac{1}{x}\), leaving us with just \(\ln{x} + 1\).

The final answer is: 
{
"Final Answer": "x(ln(x) + 1)"
}

Explanation:
The given expression can be written as 
lim_{x to ∞} (3x - ln x) / (5x + ln x)
Dividing both numerator and denominator by 'x', we get
lim_{x to ∞} (3 - 1/x) / (5 + 1/x)
This is a limit of the form lim_{a→∞}(f(a)/g(a)) where f(x) = 3 - 1/x and g(x) = 5 + 1/x. Since this limit is of type 0/0, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule.
To evaluate the above expression, we need to find the derivatives of f(x) and g(x).
f'(x) = d(3 - 1/x)/dx = (1/x^2)
g'(x) = d(5 + 1/x)/dx = (-1/x^2)
Applying L'Hospital's Rule, we have 
lim_{x to ∞} (3 - 1/x) / (5 + 1/x) = lim_{x to ∞} ((1/x^2)) / ((-1/x^2))
This limit is of type 0/0. We can again apply L'Hospital's Rule.
To evaluate the above expression, we need to find the derivatives of f(x) and g(x).
f'(x) = d((1/x^2))/dx = (-2/x^3)
g'(x) = d((-1/x^2))/dx = (2/x^3)
Applying L'Hospital's Rule again, we have 
lim_{x to ∞} ((1/x^2)) / ((-1/x^2)) = lim_{x to ∞} ((-2/x^3)) / ((2/x^3))
This limit is of type 0/0. We can again apply L'Hospital's Rule.
To evaluate the above expression, we need to find the derivatives of f(x) and g(x).
f'(x) = d((-2/x^3))/dx = (6/x^4)
g'(x) = d((2/x^3))/dx = (-6/x^4)
Applying L'Hospital's Rule again, we have 
lim_{x to ∞} ((-2/x^3)) / ((2/x^3)) = lim_{x to ∞} (6/x^4) / (-6/x^4)
Now the limit is of type ∞/∞. We can simplify it by dividing both numerator and denominator by 'x'.
lim_{x to ∞} (6/x^4) / (-6/x^4) = lim_{x to ∞} 6/x^4 * -1/(-6/x^4)
= -1/5

Here's my solution:

Let's analyze the given expression:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x}

First, we can rewrite the expression as:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} \cdot \frac{e^x}{e^x}

Now, we can simplify this expression by multiplying the numerator and denominator by $e^x$, which is the exponential function.

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x) \cdot e^x}{x \cdot e^x}

Next, we can use the fact that $\ln(a+b) \approx b$ for small values of $b$. Since $x$ is approaching zero, we can say that:

$\ln(1+x) \approx x$

So, we can simplify the expression as:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x \cdot e^x}{x \cdot e^x}

Now, we can cancel out the $x$ terms in the numerator and denominator.

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = lim_{x \to 0} e^x

As $x$ approaches zero, $e^x$ approaches 1.

So, our final answer is:

{Final Answer: "1"}

= 6x - 5

f(x) = cos(x) \* ln(x)

To find the derivative f'(x), we'll use the product rule:

f'(x) = (d(cos(x))/dx) \* ln(x) + cos(x) \* (d(ln(x))/dx)

Now, let's compute each part separately:

1. d(cos(x))/dx = -sin(x)
2. d(ln(x))/dx = 1/x

Substituting these results back into the product rule formula, we get:

f'(x) = (-sin(x)) \* ln(x) + cos(x) \* (1/x)

Simplifying this expression, we get:

f'(x) = -sin(x) \* ln(x) + cos(x)/x

So, the final answer is:

Here's my solution:

**Step 1:** Recall that the derivative of ln(x) is 1/x.

**Step 2:** Use the quotient rule to find the derivative of f(x) = (ln(x))/(x^2).

The quotient rule states that if f(x) = g(x)/h(x), then f'(x) = (g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / h(x)^2.

In this case, g(x) = ln(x) and h(x) = x^2.

**Step 3:** Find the derivatives of g(x) and h(x):

* g'(x) = d(ln(x))/dx = 1/x (from Step 1)
* h'(x) = d(x^2)/dx = 2x

**Step 4:** Plug these values into the quotient rule formula:

f'(x) = ((1/x)(x^2) - (ln(x))(2x)) / (x^2)^2
= (x - 2x ln(x)) / x^4

Simplify the expression by factoring out a 1/x from the numerator and denominator:

f'(x) = (1 - 2 ln(x))/x^3

**Final Answer:**
{Final Answer: "(1 - 2 ln(x))/x^3"}

***********************```
{
  "Step 1: Identify the function and its components"
}
The given function is f(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x).

To find the derivative, we will use the quotient rule of differentiation.

The quotient rule states that if we have a function of the form:
f(x) = g(x) / h(x)

Then the derivative f'(x) is given by:
f'(x) = (h(x)*g'(x) - g(x)*h'(x)) / (h(x))^2

In this case, g(x) = e^x and h(x) = 1 + e^x.

```
{
"Step 2: Find the derivatives of g(x) and h(x)"
}
The derivative of g(x) = e^x is g'(x) = e^x.
The derivative of h(x) = 1 + e^x is h'(x) = e^x.


```
{
"Step 3: Apply the quotient rule to find f'(x)"
}
Now we can apply the quotient rule:

f'(x) = (h(x)*g'(x) - g(x)*h'(x)) / (h(x))^2
f'(x) = ((1 + e^x)*(e^x) - e^x*(e^x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
f'(x) = ((1 + e^x)*e^x - e^(x+x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
f'(x) = (e^x + e^(2x) - e^(x+x)) / (1 + e^x)^2

Since e^(x+x) is the same as e^(2x), we can simplify:

f'(x) = (e^x + e^(2x) - e^(2x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
f'(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x)^2

```
{
"Step 4: Simplify the expression for f'(x)"
}
We can simplify further by factoring out e^x from the numerator and denominator:

f'(x) = e^x * (1/(1+e^x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
f'(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x)^2

```
{
"Step 5: Write down the final answer"
}
Final Answer:
The derivative of f(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x) is f'(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x)^2.
```

Here's the solution:

**Problem:** Find the derivative of \( \sqrt{x} \)

**Solution:**

To find the derivative, we'll use the power rule of differentiation.

Let \( f(x) = \sqrt{x} \)

Then, \( f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(\sqrt{x}) \)

Using the power rule, we know that:

\( \frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = nx^{n-1} \)

So, for \( n = 1/2 \), we have:

\( f'(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^{-1/2} \)

Simplifying the expression, we get:

\( f'(x) = \boxed{\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}} \)

**Final Answer:**

lim_(x \to \infty) \frac{3 - \frac{\ln x}{x}}{5 + \frac{\ln x}{x}} = \frac{3 - 0}{5 + 0}

The last step is to evaluate the expression.
Final Answer: 0.6}

The given expression is lim x→0 (ln(1+x))/x.
Let's analyze this limit. We can see that as x approaches 0, both the numerator and denominator approach 0.

Therefore, we can write:
lim x→0 (ln(1+x))/x = lim x→0 (∫[0,x] (1/t)dt)/x

∫[0,x] (1/t)dt = ∫[0,x] t^(-1) dt
= [-ln|t|] from 0 to x
= -ln|x| + ln|0|
We can see that ln(0) is undefined. However, we know that lim x→0 |x| = 0.
Therefore, let's rewrite the expression as:
lim x→0 (ln(1+x))/x = lim x→0 (-ln|x|)/x
Now, let's rationalize the numerator by multiplying both the numerator and denominator by -1:
lim x→0 (ln(1+x))/x = lim x→0 (-ln|x|)/x
= lim x→0 ln|x|/(-x)
To evaluate this limit, we can use the property that lim x→a f(x)/g(x) = a if lim x→a f'(x)/g'(x) exists and is equal to 1.
In our case, let's find f'(x) and g'(x):
f(x) = ln|x| => f'(x) = 1/|x|
g(x) = -x => g'(x) = -1
Now, we can check if the limit exists by evaluating lim x→0 (f'(x)/g'(x)):
lim x→0 (1/|x|/(-1)) = lim x→0 (-1/|x|) ≠ 0
This means that lim x→0 (ln|x|/(-x)) does not exist.
However, we can still find the limit by using L'Hopital's rule for limits of ratios:
lim x→a f(x)/g(x) = a if lim x→a f'(x)/g'(x) exists and is equal to 1

= -lim x→0 (ln|x|/x)

***********************{Explanation:
To find the derivative of f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4, we will use the power rule of differentiation.

Power Rule:
If f(x) = x^n, then f'(x) = nx^(n-1)

In this case, n = 2 for the term 3x^2, so we will multiply it by 2.
For the term -5x, n = 1, so we will multiply it by 1.
The constant term 4 has a power of 0 (any number to the power of 0 is 1), so its derivative is 0.

Differentiating each term:
d(3x^2)/dx = 3*2*x^(2-1) = 6x
d(-5x)/dx = -5*1*x^(1-1) = -5
d(4)/dx = 0

Now, we add the results of differentiating each term to get the derivative:
f'(x) = 6x - 5}

{Final Answer: "6x - 5"}

***********************{Explanation:
"The derivative of a function is another function that represents the rate of change of the original function with respect to one of its variables. In this case, we are given the function f(x) = sin x and we need to find its derivative.

To solve this problem, we will use the definition of the derivative:

f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [f(x + h) - f(x)] / h

For the function f(x) = sin x, we can substitute it into the definition of the derivative and simplify:

f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(sin (x + h) - sin x)] / h

Using the trigonometric identity for the sine of a sum, we get:

sin (x + h) = sin x cos h + cos x sin h

Substituting this into our expression, we get:

f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(sin x cos h + cos x sin h - sin x)] / h

Simplifying the numerator and canceling out the sin x term, we get:

f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [cos x sin h] / h

As h approaches 0, this expression approaches cos x.

Therefore, the derivative of f(x) = sin x is:

Final Answer: cos(x)}

***********************{Explanation:
Step 1: First, we need to find the first derivative of F(x) which is F'(x).
To do this, we'll apply the power rule of differentiation which states that if f(x) = x^n, then f'(x) = nx^(n-1).
So, the first derivative of F(x) will be:
F'(x) = d/dx (x^4 - 3x^3 + x)
= 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1

F''(x) = d/dx (4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1)
= 12x^2 - 18x

***********************{Explanation: To find the derivative of f(x) = tan x, we'll use the definition of a derivative and the properties of trigonometric functions.
Step 1: Recall the definition of the derivative
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [f(x + h) - f(x)]/h

Step 2: Apply this definition to f(x) = tan x
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(tan(x + h) - tan x)/h]

Step 3: Use the identity for tan(A + B)
tan(x + h) = (tan x + tan h)/(1 - tan x tan h)

Step 4: Substitute this identity into our derivative
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [((tan x + tan h)/(1 - tan x tan h)) - tan x]/h

Step 5: Multiply the numerator and denominator of the fraction by (1 - tan x tan h)
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(tan x + tan h)(1 - tan x tan h) - tan x(1 - tan x tan h)]/h(1 - tan x tan h)

Step 6: Simplify the numerator
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [tan x - tan x tan x tan h + tan h - tan x tan h]/h(1 - tan x tan h)

Step 7: Combine like terms in the numerator
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(tan x - tan x tan x tan h + tan h)/h] / (1 - tan x tan h)

Step 8: Factor out a tan h from the first two terms of the numerator
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [tan h(1 - tan x tan h) + tan h]/h / (1 - tan x tan h)

Step 9: Add tan h to the numerator and combine like terms
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [2 tan h/(1 - tan x tan h)]/h / (1 - tan x tan h)

Step 10: Cancel out the factor of h in both numerator and denominator
f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [2 tan h] / (1 - tan x tan h)^2

Step 11: Evaluate this limit as h approaches zero
f'(x) = 2 tan x / (1 - tan^2 x)
 
Recall the identity sin^2(x) + cos^2(x) = 1
sin^2(x) = 1 - cos^2(x)

Step 12: Substitute this identity into our derivative
f'(x) = 2 tan x / (cos^2 x)
 
Apply the identity 1/cos(x) = sec(x)
sec^2(x) = 1 / cos^2(x)

Step 13: Rewrite our derivative using this identity
f'(x) = 2 tan x * sec^2(x)

Recall that sin(x)/cos(x) is equal to tan(x), so multiply both sides by cos(x)/sin(x)
cos(x)*sec^2(x) = 1/sin(x)*sec^2(x)
 
Multiply the denominator and numerator of the second term on the right-hand side by sin^2 x
cos(x)*sec^2(x) = (cos(x)/sin(x)) * sec^2(x) * sin^2(x)

Recall that cos(x)/sin(x) is equal to 1/tan(x), so substitute this identity into our equation
cos(x)*sec^2(x) = 1/tan(x) * sec^2(x) * sin^2(x)
 
Multiply both sides by tan(x) and rearrange terms
cos(x)tan(x)sec^2(x) = sec^2(x)

Divide both sides of the equation by sec^2(x)
cos(x)tan(x) = 1
 
Recall that cos(x)/sin(x) is equal to cotangent (cot), so substitute this identity into our equation
cot(x) * tan(x) = 1

Step 14: Use this final form in your answer.

Explanation:
To solve this problem, we will use a technique called logarithmic differentiation. This involves taking the natural logarithm of both sides of the equation and then differentiating implicitly.

Let's start by writing:

x^x = y

Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives us:

ln(x^x) = ln(y)

Using the property of logarithms that states ln(a^b) = b*ln(a), we can rewrite this as:

x*ln(x) = ln(y)

Now, let's differentiate both sides with respect to x:

(1 + ln(x))*d(ln(x))/dx = 1/y * dy/dx

Using the chain rule for differentiation, we get:

(1 + ln(x)) * (1/x^2*x) = 1/y * dy/dx

Now, substitute y = x^x back into the equation and simplify:

(1 + ln(x)) * (1/x) = x^(x-1)

This is the final expression for the derivative of x^x with respect to x. Note that this expression cannot be simplified further using elementary functions.

Final Answer:
No closed-form solution, but we can use logarithmic differentiation to get an expression for the derivative: 

{x*(ln(x) + 1)}

Using the product rule for differentiation, we can write:

= x^2 d/dx (e^x) + e^x d/dx (x^2)
= x^2 e^x + e^x d/dx (x^2)

d/dx (x^2) = 2x

F'(x) = x^2 e^x + 2xe^x
= e^x (x^2 + 2x)
= e^x (x(x+2))

d/dx (x^2 + 2x) = 2x + 2

= e^x (2x + 2 + x^2(x+2))
= e^x (2x + 2 + x^3 + 2x^2)

= x^3(1/x) + (ln x)(3x^2)
= x^2 + 3x^3\ln x

= 2x + 3x^2(1/x) + \ln x(9x^2)
= 2x + 3x + 9x^3\ln x

Here's the solution:

**Problem:** \( \frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \right) \)

**Solution:**

To solve this problem, we will use the Quotient Rule of differentiation. The Quotient Rule states that if we have a function of the form:

\( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \)

then its derivative is given by:

\( f'(x) = \frac{h(x) g'(x) - g(x) h'(x)}{(h(x))^2} \)

In this case, we have:

\( g(x) = \sin x \)
\( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

**Step 1:** Find the derivatives of \(g(x)\) and \(h(x)\):

\( g'(x) = \cos x \)
\( h'(x) = 2x \)

**Step 2:** Apply the Quotient Rule:

\( f'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1)(\cos x) - (\sin x)(2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \)

Simplifying, we get:

\( f'(x) = \frac{x^2 \cos x + \cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \)

This is the final answer.

***********************{Explanation: First, we'll use the chain rule to find the first derivative F'(x) = d(e^(x^2))/dx = e^(x^2) * d(x^2)/dx = 2xe^(x^2). Now, we need to find the second derivative. We can do this by differentiating the expression for the first derivative with respect to x again.

Let's start by defining y = F'(x) = 2xe^(x^2), which is the first derivative of e^(x^2).

To find the second derivative, we'll use the product rule: d(y)/dx = d(2xe^(x^2))/dx. We can expand this expression as follows:

d(2xe^(x^2))/dx = 2e^(x^2) * d(x)/dx + x * d(e^(x^2))/dx.

Now, we'll use the chain rule to find d(e^(x^2))/dx:

d(e^(x^2))/dx = e^(x^2) * d(x^2)/dx = 2xe^(x^2).

So, the expression for the second derivative simplifies to:

d(2xe^(x^2))/dx = 2e^(x^2) + x * 2xe^(x^2)

= 2e^(x^2) (1 + x^2).

Therefore, the second derivative of F(x) = e^(x^2) is:

F''(x) = 2e^(x^2) (1 + x^2).}

{Final Answer: "2e^(x^2) (1 + x^2)"}
